<a id="lesson-9"></a>

## Manuscript 9 — s3-mat-y5-w01-l1

**Existing title:** Reading and writing numbers to the millions  
**Source:** `docs/curriculum-authoring/lessons/s3-mat-y5-w01-l1.md`  
**Authoring status:** prose_rewrite_for_review, owner review pending.

# Year 5 Mathematics — Reading and writing numbers to the millions

**Lesson ID:** `s3-mat-y5-w01-l1`  
**Existing title:** Reading and writing numbers to the millions  
**Existing unit:** Whole numbers to the millions  
**Year / stage:** Year 5 / Stage 3 · Week 1, Lesson 1  
**Existing attached learning outcomes:** Preserve the outcome metadata already attached to the Coming Soon record. The source migration's `outcome_codes` array is empty; no substitute code is invented here.  
**Estimated learning time:** 55–65 minutes, including pauses to practise  
**Materials:** pencil and paper or an accessible on-screen writing space  
**Status:** Rewritten, prose-first instructional manuscript for owner review. Not approved for publishing.  
**Media:** static diagrams only. No videos. **No reflections.**

---

# The museum with a million tiny treasures

Imagine a museum that collects tiny objects: ancient beads, polished stones and miniature fossils. A new shipment has arrived, and the curator has written a number on the crate:

## 2,405,018

The curator needs to telephone another museum and say exactly how many objects arrived. She cannot simply point at the digits. She has to turn that long numeral into words that someone else can understand.

How would you say it?

You might recognise the **2** at the beginning and the **18** at the end. But what does the **405** in the middle mean? Why are there commas? And why doesn't the curator say every zero?

By the end of this lesson, you will be able to read numbers like this confidently and write them down when someone says them aloud. You will learn a method that works even when a number has several zeros, because those are often the numbers that trick us.

## Part 1 — What the commas are telling you

You already know how to read a three-digit number. For example, **405** is *four hundred and five*. The 4 tells us there are four hundreds, the 0 tells us there are no tens, and the 5 tells us there are five ones.

A long number is easier to understand when we split it into **groups of three digits**, starting at the right-hand end.

Look again at the museum's number:

**2 | 405 | 018**

The three groups have different jobs.

| Millions | Thousands | Ones |
|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| **2** | **405** | **018** |

The group on the far right tells us how many **ones** there are. The group in the middle tells us how many **thousands** there are. The group on the left tells us how many **millions** there are.

We read the groups **from left to right**, saying the name of the group as we go.

Start with the left group: **2 million**.

Move to the middle group: **405 thousand**, which we say as **four hundred and five thousand**.

Finish with the right group: **018**. Although it is written using three positions in the chart, its value is just **eighteen**. The zero at the front does not make it “zero hundred and eighteen”.

Put those parts together:

**2,405,018 = two million, four hundred and five thousand, eighteen.**

Notice something important: we did **not** read the whole string digit by digit. Saying “two, four, zero, five, zero, one, eight” would describe the symbols, but it would not tell the curator the number of objects in the usual way.

### Why does each group have three places?

Within a group, the positions are **hundreds, tens and ones**. After three places, we move into the next group of a thousand.

One thousand is 1,000 ones. One million is 1,000 thousands.

That is why the groups have names. The same familiar three-digit reading skill can be reused again and again. You read the digits in a group, then add **thousand** or **million** when that group needs a name.

A comma is a **separator**, not a digit. It helps you see where one group ends and the next begins. You do not say “comma” when reading the number aloud.

**Illustration A — required:** Show `2,405,018` above three aligned labelled blocks: `2` (millions), `405` (thousands), `018` (ones). Under each block show its spoken contribution: “two million”, “four hundred and five thousand”, “eighteen”. The diagram must be mathematically accurate and legible on a small screen. **Alt text:** “The number 2,405,018 is divided into three groups: 2 millions, 405 thousands and 018 ones. It is read two million, four hundred and five thousand, eighteen.”

### Try this with me

Read **3,216,407**. First, cover the last two groups with your hand or imagine looking at only the first group. You see **3**. That is **three million**.

Now look at **216**. You already know how to say that: **two hundred and sixteen**. Because it is in the thousands group, it becomes **two hundred and sixteen thousand**.

Finally, read **407**: **four hundred and seven**.

The complete number is **three million, two hundred and sixteen thousand, four hundred and seven**.

**Check question:** In **3,216,407**, does the middle group mean two hundred and sixteen, or two hundred and sixteen **thousand**?

**Answer:** Two hundred and sixteen thousand. Its position gives the group its name.

If you said only “two hundred and sixteen”, you read the digits correctly but forgot the group's value. The correction is simple: look at the label above the group before you say it.

---

## Part 2 — Zeros have a job, even when we do not say them

Some large numbers look especially confusing because they contain zeros. Consider:

## 6,032,409

Split the number into groups:

**6 | 032 | 409**

The millions group is **6**, so we begin with **six million**.

The thousands group is **032**. You may be tempted to say “zero hundred and thirty-two thousand”. We do not need to do that. The zero tells us there are no hundreds *within that group*. The group represents **32 thousand**, so we say **thirty-two thousand**.

The ones group is **409**, which is **four hundred and nine**.

So the full reading is:

**Six million, thirty-two thousand, four hundred and nine.**

The zeros have not disappeared from the *written* number. They are still needed to keep each digit in the correct place. But when speaking, we usually say the value of each group rather than naming its empty positions.

Now consider a different number:

## 9,000,705

This time the middle group is **000**. There are **no thousands at all**. We do not say “zero thousand”. We skip that group when reading aloud.

We say:

**Nine million, seven hundred and five.**

This is a powerful rule: **if an entire group is 000, you do not need to say that group aloud.**

But you **must** keep its three zeros when writing the numeral. Without them, **9,000,705** could be mistaken for **9,705**, which is only nine thousand, seven hundred and five. The difference is enormous.

### Compare these two numbers carefully

**4,005,080** means **four million, five thousand, eighty**.

**4,050,080** means **four million, fifty thousand, eighty**.

The digits look similar, but the middle groups are different: **005** and **050**. In the first number, the 5 is in the thousands position. In the second, it is in the ten-thousands position. That is why one number has five thousand and the other has fifty thousand.

**Illustration B — required:** Two horizontal number-group diagrams labelled `4 | 005 | 080` and `4 | 050 | 080`. Highlight the position of the 5 within the thousands group in each number. Label the contributions “5 thousand” and “50 thousand”. **Alt text:** “4,005,080 contains five thousand, while 4,050,080 contains fifty thousand because the digit 5 is in a different position.”

### Pause and answer

Read **7,004,020** aloud.

Do not rush. Find the millions group, then the thousands group, then the ones group.

**Answer:** **Seven million, four thousand, twenty.**

**If you said “seven million, forty thousand, twenty”:** look at the middle group. It is **004**, not **040**. Four in the rightmost position of the thousands group means **four thousand**.

**If you said “seven million, four thousand, two hundred”:** look at the final group. **020** is twenty. **200** would be two hundred.

---

## Part 3 — Turning spoken numbers back into digits

Reading a number is one direction of the skill. Writing a numeral when someone says it aloud is the reverse direction.

Suppose the museum curator says:

> “Four million, seventy thousand, six hundred and two.”

How do you write that correctly?

The safest method is to make three spaces for the groups:

| Millions | Thousands | Ones |
|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| ? | ??? | ??? |

First, listen for **million**. The curator said **four million**, so put **4** in the millions group.

Next, listen for **thousand**. She said **seventy thousand**. Seventy is written **70**, but the thousands group needs **three positions** because it sits between the millions and ones groups. Write it as **070**.

Finally, listen for the part after “thousand”: **six hundred and two**. That is **602**.

Now join the groups with commas:

**4 | 070 | 602 → 4,070,602.**

Read it back to check: four million, seventy thousand, six hundred and two. It matches exactly.

### Why do we write 070 instead of just 70?

Imagine writing the groups without preserving their positions: **4 | 70 | 602**. When those digits are pushed together, you risk producing a numeral that has the wrong number of places.

The middle and right groups must each have **three digits** when there is a group to their left. Writing 070 makes the seventy stay in the correct part of the thousands group.

### Worked example — a number with almost no spoken parts

Write **“two million and nine”** as a numeral.

There are two million, **no thousands**, and nine ones.

| Millions | Thousands | Ones |
|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| 2 | 000 | 009 |

So the number is **2,000,009**.

It is **not** 2,009. That would mean two thousand and nine, because it has no millions group.

### Worked example — a number without millions

Write **“nine hundred and twelve thousand, six”**.

There is no millions group. The thousands group is **912**. The ones group is **006** because the spoken final amount is six.

The numeral is **912,006**.

We do not need to add a leading `000,` millions group. We simply start at the largest nonzero group.

### Try it before revealing the answer

Write **“five million, twelve thousand, three”**.

Think in groups: five million | twelve thousand | three ones.

**Answer: 5,012,003.**

If you wrote **5,12,3**, the values are in the right order, but the group positions have not been preserved. Write **012** for the thousands group and **003** for the ones group.

---

## Part 4 — Guided practice: let the groups do the work

These questions begin with a little support and then ask you to do more of the thinking yourself. For each one, enter your answer before opening the explanation.

### Guided question 1: read the groups

**Number:** 8,104,030.

The groups are **8 | 104 | 030**.

What do we say for the middle group?

**Answer:** **One hundred and four thousand.**

Now say the entire number.

**Full answer:** **Eight million, one hundred and four thousand, thirty.**

**If you said “three hundred” at the end:** the final group is 030, not 300. A 3 in the tens position means thirty.

### Guided question 2: fill the missing group

A number is read as **“three million, forty thousand, seven.”**

| Millions | Thousands | Ones |
|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| 3 | ___ | 007 |

What belongs in the thousands group?

**Answer:** **040**. The complete number is **3,040,007**.

**Hint:** Forty thousand means forty groups of one thousand, so the thousands group must read “forty”.

**If you wrote 400:** that would mean four hundred thousand, ten times too much.

### Guided question 3: explain the difference

Which numeral means **“six million, five thousand, ninety”**?

A. 6,050,090  
B. 6,005,090  
C. 6,500,090

**Answer: B — 6,005,090.**

In B, the middle group is 005, which means five thousand. In A it is 050, or fifty thousand. In C it is 500, or five hundred thousand. The final group 090 means ninety in all three numbers.

### Guided question 4: repair a museum label

A label says:

**1,060,300 — “one million, six hundred thousand, three hundred.”**

Is the label accurate?

**Answer:** No. The middle group is **060**, so it means **sixty thousand**, not six hundred thousand.

The corrected reading is **one million, sixty thousand, three hundred**.

To check, compare the two possible thousands groups: **060** is sixty; **600** is six hundred.

### Guided question 5: write without a model

Write **“seven million, two hundred and three thousand, forty-one”**.

**Answer:** **7,203,041.**

The millions group is 7, the thousands group is 203, and the ones group is 041. The zero in 041 is needed because forty-one has no hundreds.

---

## Part 5 — Independent practice: the curator's number cards

You now have the method. Work through the following number cards **without looking back at the worked examples**. The answers and explanations are provided after the tasks so you can check your work.

### Card set A — numerals into words

**1.** 4,020,106  
**2.** 8,300,009  
**3.** 705,040  
**4.** 2,002,002  
**5.** 9,090,090  
**6.** 1,000,001

### Card set B — words into numerals

**7.** Six million, eight thousand, thirty  
**8.** Three million, four hundred thousand, five  
**9.** One million, ninety thousand, seventy-one  
**10.** Nine hundred and six thousand, four hundred and ten  
**11.** Seven million, seven thousand, seven  
**12.** Five million, fifty thousand, fifty

### Card set C — explain the error

**13.** A learner says that **3,006,040** is “three million, sixty thousand, forty”. What is wrong, and what is the correct reading?

**14.** A learner hears “eight million, four hundred and two” and writes **8,402**. Explain the mistake and write the correct numeral.

**15.** A learner says **920,005** has “nine hundred and twenty million” because the first group has three digits. Explain why that is not correct.

### Answers and teaching feedback

**1.** Four million, twenty thousand, one hundred and six. The thousands group is 020, not 200.

**2.** Eight million, three hundred thousand, nine. The last group 009 is nine.

**3.** Seven hundred and five thousand, forty. There is no millions group; 705 is the thousands group.

**4.** Two million, two thousand and two. The middle 002 means two thousand; the final 002 means two ones.

**5.** Nine million, ninety thousand, ninety. Both 090 groups are read “ninety”, but one represents thousands and the other ones.

**6.** One million and one. The 000 thousands group is not spoken.

**7.** 6,008,030. The thousands group is 008 and the ones group is 030.

**8.** 3,400,005. Four hundred thousand is 400 in the thousands group; five is 005 in the ones group.

**9.** 1,090,071. Ninety thousand is 090 in the thousands group; seventy-one is 071 in the ones group.

**10.** 906,410. There is no millions group; the thousands group is 906.

**11.** 7,007,007. Both the thousands and ones groups are 007, but they represent different amounts.

**12.** 5,050,050. Both smaller groups are 050, and both need their leading zero.

**13.** The middle group is 006, meaning **six thousand**, not sixty thousand. Correct reading: **three million, six thousand, forty**.

**14.** The learner left out the zero thousands group. “Eight million, four hundred and two” is **8,000,402**. The numeral 8,402 means eight thousand, four hundred and two.

**15.** The first group is not automatically millions. In **920,005**, there are only two groups: 920 thousands and 005 ones. Correct reading: **nine hundred and twenty thousand, five**.

**If several answers were wrong:** Check whether the errors came from reading the *wrong group name* or placing digits in the *wrong positions*. For group-name errors, point to the labels “millions / thousands / ones” before reading. For position errors, rebuild the three-digit groups before joining them.

---

## Part 6 — Application: save the museum's exhibition labels

The museum is preparing three displays. The counts below are fictional, but the numerals must be accurate.

**Display A:** 1,205,030 miniature shells  
**Display B:** 980,006 polished stones  
**Display C:** 3,040,080 ancient beads

The curator needs printed labels that visitors can read aloud.

### Task A — write the exhibition labels

Write each count in words, using the correct group names.

**Model answers:**

- **A:** One million, two hundred and five thousand, thirty.
- **B:** Nine hundred and eighty thousand, six.
- **C:** Three million, forty thousand, eighty.

### Task B — catch a costly error

A draft label for Display C reads:

> “Three million, four hundred thousand, eighty.”

The numeral printed beside it is **3,040,080**.

Explain exactly why the words and numeral do not match.

**Model answer:** The numeral's thousands group is **040**, meaning **forty thousand**. “Four hundred thousand” would require **400** in the thousands group and would make the numeral **3,400,080**. The draft label is wrong because it gives the digit 4 the wrong place value.

**Feedback if the student only writes “the zero is wrong”:** Ask them to name the two different values—40,000 and 400,000—and show which group matches each value.

### Task C — a telephone message

Another museum calls and says:

> “Please reserve space for two million, three thousand and sixteen specimens.”

Write the number as digits, then explain how you protected the empty positions.

**Model answer:** **2,003,016**. The groups are 2 millions, 003 thousands and 016 ones. The leading zeros in the smaller groups hold the 3 in the thousands position and the 16 in the ones group.

### Task D — make a matching pair

Create your own seven-digit number that contains at least **two zero digits**, and write its name in words.

**Example:** **5,006,207 — five million, six thousand, two hundred and seven.**

**Marking guide:** Accept any seven-digit whole number whose word form matches its groups and that contains at least two zero digits. The child must supply both a numeral and its matching word form. A response with only a numeral is incomplete.

---

## Part 7 — Check what you can do independently

This is the final check. Complete it without hints. You can review explanations afterwards, and you may retry any missed question.

**Question 1 (2 points).** Write **5,070,009** in words.

**Question 2 (2 points).** Write **“two million, six thousand, fourteen”** in digits.

**Question 3 (2 points).** A student says **4,005,080** means “four million, fifty thousand, eighty”. Explain the mistake and give the correct reading.

**Question 4 (2 points).** Write **900,090** in words.

**Question 5 (2 points).** The words **“seven million, forty thousand, six”** are written as **7,400,006**. Is that correct? If not, write the correct numeral and explain why.

### Answer key and marking

**1.** **Five million, seventy thousand, nine.** Award 2 points for all groups correct; 1 if only one group is misread. The middle group 070 is seventy thousand.

**2.** **2,006,014.** Award 2 for the correct numeral; 1 if the group values are identified correctly but a zero placeholder is misplaced.

**3.** **Four million, five thousand, eighty.** The middle group 005 means five thousand, not fifty thousand. Award 1 for the corrected reading and 1 for explaining the difference between 005 and 050.

**4.** **Nine hundred thousand, ninety.** Award 2 for correct reading; 1 for correctly identifying the thousands but misreading 090.

**5.** **Not correct.** The correct numeral is **7,040,006**. The spoken “forty thousand” belongs in the middle group as 040, not 400. Award 1 for the correct numeral and 1 for the explanation.

**Total: 10 points.** A score of 9–10 suggests readiness to move on. A score of 7–8 suggests a short targeted retry. A score below 7 suggests working through the grouped-number examples again with support. These are practical teaching thresholds, not standardised assessment scores.

### Targeted second chances

If you struggled **reading** numbers, try **3,004,020**. The answer is **three million, four thousand, twenty**. Check each group against its label.

If you struggled **writing** numbers, try **“five million, twelve thousand, three”**. The answer is **5,012,003**. Write 5 | 012 | 003 before joining the groups.

If you struggled with **zero placeholders**, compare **6,005,040** and **6,050,040**. The first is **six million, five thousand, forty**; the second is **six million, fifty thousand, forty**. The different positions of the 5 change its value.

**Completion:** Save the final check and show the correct explanations for any missed items. The next mathematics lesson will build on these number groups to investigate the value of each individual digit.

---

# Cursor implementation appendix — not student-facing

**Purpose:** Implement the manuscript above without rewriting, compressing or substituting its instructional content. Preserve the existing Coming Soon lesson's title, ID and attached learning outcomes.

**Suggested layout:** Short, readable lesson panels following the instructional progression; substantial teaching paragraphs remain visible and readable, not collapsed into bullet cards. Show diagrams beside the explanation they support. Guided questions may use “try → hint → submit → explanation” states. Independent practice should allow written working and an explicit answer-reveal/check stage. Do not show answer keys before the learner attempts the relevant task.

**Visual assets:** Two static diagrams specified in Parts 1 and 2. Use accurate typography and column alignment. No generated decorative graphics are required. Pip/Juno are optional and must not interrupt the teaching.

**Input handling:** For word-form answers, accept reasonable Australian-English variations in punctuation, commas, hyphens and use of “and” without changing mathematical meaning. For numeral answers, normalise optional commas and spaces but reject different digit values. Free-response explanations in Questions 3 and 5 and Application Tasks B–D need a visible model answer/rubric or adult review; do not claim that simplistic keyword matching accurately grades reasoning.

**Accessibility:** All content navigable by keyboard and screen reader; diagram alt text provided; group labels available in text; no colour-only instructions; readable contrast and responsive tables.

**Data and feedback:** Save attempts and completion; keep the first answer available when retrying; show feedback that names the mistaken group or placeholder. Do not add a reflection step.

**Editorial acceptance:** The owner must review this prose-first rewrite before it becomes the pattern for the other 19,201 lesson manuscripts.

<a id="lesson-10"></a>

## Manuscript 10 — s3-mat-y5-w01-l2

**Existing title:** Place value charts and expanded form  
**Source:** `docs/curriculum-authoring/lessons/s3-mat-y5-w01-l2.md`  
**Authoring status:** prose_draft_for_review, owner review pending.

# The secret job of every digit

**Year 5 · Mathematics · Week 1 Lesson 2 · 60 minutes.** Keep existing outcome metadata. Student-facing teaching follows. No reflections.

## Why 4 can mean four different things

A museum has four cabinets. One contains **4** objects, another **40**, another **400**, and the last **4,000**. Every label contains the digit 4, yet the cabinets hold very different amounts. A digit does not tell us its value on its own. **Its position tells us what kind of units we are counting.**

You already know that ten ones make one ten, and ten tens make one hundred. This pattern never stops. Ten hundreds make one thousand; ten thousands make ten thousand; ten hundred-thousands make one million. Each move one place left multiplies a digit's value by ten. Each move one place right divides its value by ten.

Look at **3,205,040**. We can put every digit into its own labelled place.

| Millions | Hundred-thousands | Ten-thousands | Thousands | Hundreds | Tens | Ones |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 3 | 2 | 0 | 5 | 0 | 4 | 0 |

The 3 means **3,000,000**, not 3. The 2 means **200,000**, not 20,000. The 5 means **5,000**, and the 4 means **40**. Zeros show places where there are no units. They may not add anything, but they prevent other digits from sliding into the wrong columns.

## A number can be taken apart without changing it

Imagine emptying the museum cabinets into piles. We can describe the whole collection by naming each pile and adding them together. This is called **expanded form**. For our number, we write:

**3,205,040 = 3,000,000 + 200,000 + 5,000 + 40.**

Notice that the plus signs mean the values are combined. We do not write 3 + 2 + 5 + 4, because that would total only 14. We must add the *values* represented by the digits.

Let's expand **6,070,203** together. Begin with six million: 6,000,000. The hundred-thousands column contains zero, so there is no hundred-thousands term. Seven ten-thousands contribute 70,000. Two hundreds contribute 200, and three ones contribute 3. Put them together: **6,000,000 + 70,000 + 200 + 3**. Recombine to check: six million and seventy thousand make 6,070,000; add 200 and 3 to get 6,070,203.

We can also travel backwards. Suppose we have **2,000,000 + 400,000 + 30,000 + 6,000 + 80 + 1**. Write the digits in the columns they belong to: 2 millions, 4 hundred-thousands, 3 ten-thousands, 6 thousands, **0 hundreds**, 8 tens and 1 one. The numeral is **2,436,081**. The zero in the hundreds column matters: without it, the 8 and 1 would move into the wrong places.

### A tempting mistake

Someone expands **5,004,090** as 5,000,000 + 4,000 + 900. They have noticed the correct digits, but the last term is wrong. In 5,004,090, the 9 is in the **tens** column, so its value is **90**. The correct expansion is **5,000,000 + 4,000 + 90**. When uncertain, read the column headings rather than counting zeros from memory.

## Learn with me

**1.** What is the value of the 3 in 8,304,012? Think about the column: it is the hundred-thousands column, so the answer is **300,000**. If you answered 30,000, you moved the digit one place too far right.

**2.** Complete 7,050,400 = 7,000,000 + ___ + ___. The 5 represents **50,000** and the 4 represents **400**.

**3.** Rebuild 4,000,000 + 200,000 + 7,000 + 60 + 5. Place each value in the chart, including the empty columns. The answer is **4,207,065**.

**4.** Why are 2,040,000 and 2,400,000 different? In the first, 4 means **40,000**; in the second, it means **400,000**. A single move left makes the digit ten times more valuable.

## Your turn: work independently

Write the value of the named digit or the complete expansion before checking.

**A.** Value of 6 in **2,650,008**: **600,000**.  
**B.** Value of 4 in **7,004,500**: **4,000**.  
**C.** Expand **1,208,040**: **1,000,000 + 200,000 + 8,000 + 40**.  
**D.** Expand **9,030,506**: **9,000,000 + 30,000 + 500 + 6**.  
**E.** Rebuild **6,000,000 + 100,000 + 5,000 + 9**: **6,105,009**.  
**F.** Rebuild **3,000,000 + 80,000 + 700 + 20 + 4**: **3,080,724**.

If your answer has too few digits, draw all seven place-value columns and enter zero in each empty place. If a term is ten times too large, check whether you read the column to the left of the correct one.

## Apply the skill: an incorrect library record

A library database shows **4,206,015** pages. Another screen accidentally shows **4,260,015**. Both have the same digits, but the number has changed. The first contains 6,000; the second contains 60,000. The difference is **54,000 pages**. This is why place value matters beyond the classroom: shifting a digit can create a major recording error.

**Task:** Expand the correct number, then explain the difference between the records. **Model:** 4,000,000 + 200,000 + 6,000 + 10 + 5; the 6 has shifted from thousands to ten-thousands, increasing the total by 54,000.

## Final check — answer without looking back

**1 (2 marks).** Expand 8,006,040. **Answer:** 8,000,000 + 6,000 + 40.  
**2 (2).** Rebuild 2,000,000 + 90,000 + 300 + 8. **Answer:** 2,090,308.  
**3 (2).** Explain why 6,003,050 is not 6,000,000 + 3,000 + 500. **Answer:** The 5 is tens, so the last term is 50.  
**4 (2).** Name the value of 7 in 5,070,200. **Answer:** 70,000.

**Scoring:** 7–8 ready to continue; 5–6 redo the missed type; 0–4 rebuild each number in the chart. Retry: 4,020,305 expands to 4,000,000 + 20,000 + 300 + 5.

**Illustration:** Accurate seven-column place-value grid for 3,205,040, with each nonzero digit connected to its expanded value. Alt text must name all columns and values. Cursor: retain prose and worked steps, support keyboard entry, save attempts and provide targeted feedback. No reflection.

<a id="lesson-11"></a>

## Manuscript 11 — s3-mat-y5-w01-l3

**Existing title:** Comparing and ordering large numbers  
**Source:** `docs/curriculum-authoring/lessons/s3-mat-y5-w01-l3.md`  
**Authoring status:** prose_draft_for_review, owner review pending.

# Which number is really bigger?

**Year 5 · Mathematics · Week 1 Lesson 3 · 60 minutes.** Keep existing attached outcomes.

## Two enormous collections

The imaginary Ocean Museum has **4,305,090** shells. The Mountain Museum has **4,350,009** stones. Both numbers begin with four million. Both contain the digits 3 and 5. It is tempting to look for the largest digit anywhere in each number, but that does not work. A digit's position matters more than its appearance.

To compare numbers, begin at the **largest place value**, on the left. The first digit in both numbers is 4, meaning four million, so neither is ahead yet. The next digits are both 3, meaning three hundred thousand. Still equal. At the ten-thousands column, however, Ocean has **0** and Mountain has **5**. Mountain has fifty thousand more in that column. No combination of digits in smaller columns can reverse the result, because all those smaller positions together are worth less than one full unit of the next larger position when comparing equal-length whole numbers at their first difference.

We can write **4,305,090 < 4,350,009**. The symbol **<** means *is less than*. Turn the statement around and we get **4,350,009 > 4,305,090**, where **>** means *is greater than*. The open side faces the larger number, but it is safer to read the sentence aloud than to rely on remembering the symbol's shape.

## Why we compare from the left

Think about comparing two amounts of money: one person has six million dollars, another has five million dollars. Even if the second person has many thousands and hundreds as well, five million plus all the smaller places cannot reach six million when each smaller digit is at most 9. This is why the **first different digit from the left** settles the comparison.

Watch this method with **7,208,600** and **7,280,060**. Both have 7 millions and 2 hundred-thousands. In the ten-thousands place, the first has 0 and the second has 8. Therefore **7,208,600 < 7,280,060**. We do not need to compare the hundreds or tens.

Now compare **3,405,800** and **3,405,080**. The numbers match through the thousands column. At hundreds, the first has 8 and the second has 0. So **3,405,800 > 3,405,080**. Sometimes you must look quite far right before finding the deciding place.

If the two numerals are identical, we use **=**, meaning equal. We can also compare a numeral with expanded form: **2,060,004 = 2,000,000 + 60,000 + 4**. Different writing does not necessarily mean different value.

## Ordering more than two numbers

Imagine four libraries reporting visitor totals: North **2,405,600**, East **2,450,060**, West **2,405,060** and South **2,540,006**. We need them in **ascending order**, from smallest to largest.

All four numbers have two million. South has five hundred-thousands, while the others have four, so South is the largest. Among the others, East has five ten-thousands, while North and West have zero, so East comes after those two. Finally, North and West match until the hundreds column: North has 6 hundreds and West has 0. That makes West smaller.

The ascending order is **West, North, East, South**. In numerals: **2,405,060; 2,405,600; 2,450,060; 2,540,006**.

**Descending order** means the reverse: largest to smallest. The descending list is South, East, North, West. A reliable check is to compare each neighbouring pair. In an ascending list, every number must be no smaller than the one before it.

## Practise with support

**1.** Which is greater: 9,010,005 or 9,001,500? The millions match, and the hundred-thousands match. At ten-thousands, 1 is greater than 0. **Answer: 9,010,005 > 9,001,500.**

**2.** Compare 4,672,105 and 4,671,999. The first difference is at **thousands**: 2 thousands versus 1 thousand. **Answer: 4,672,105 > 4,671,999.**

**3.** Is 10,000,000 greater than 9,999,999? Yes. The first number has eight digits and the second has seven. A positive whole number with more digits is larger, provided we are not counting leading zeros.

**4.** Put 6,070,500; 6,007,500; 6,700,050 in ascending order. Compare from the left, not from the final three digits. **Answer: 6,007,500; 6,070,500; 6,700,050.**

**5.** A student says 5,809,004 < 5,790,999 because “790,999 looks bigger than 809,004”. Explain the mistake. **Model:** Both have five million. At hundred-thousands, 8 is greater than 7, so **5,809,004 > 5,790,999**. The comparison must start with the greatest place.

## Independent problems

Write your answer and the place that decides it.

**A.** 7,030,040 ___ 7,003,400. **>**, ten-thousands.  
**B.** 12,000,009 ___ 11,999,999. **>**, millions after matching ten-millions.  
**C.** 6,400,070 ___ 6,000,000 + 400,000 + 70. **=**.  
**D.** Order 8,080,008; 8,800,080; 8,008,800; 8,080,080 ascending. **8,008,800; 8,080,008; 8,080,080; 8,800,080.**  
**E.** Order 3,905,010; 3,950,001; 3,590,100; 3,905,100 descending. **3,950,001; 3,905,100; 3,905,010; 3,590,100.**  
**F.** Give a number strictly between 5,060,000 and 5,070,000. **Example:** 5,065,000. Any whole number from 5,060,001 to 5,069,999 works.  
**G.** Is “4,999,999 is greater than 5,000,000 because it contains six nines” correct? **No.** Five million exceeds four million, regardless of the smaller digits.

## Real-world decision: the science archive

Four imaginary observatories processed these signals: Aurora **14,205,090**; Beacon **14,250,009**; Comet **14,205,900**; Delta **14,025,990**.

First, order them from fewest to most. **Answer: Delta, Aurora, Comet, Beacon.** Next, decide whether Beacon processed more than fifteen million. **No:** 14,250,009 is less than 15,000,000. Finally, find which two counts are closest. **Aurora and Comet**, separated by **810** signals. Subtracting 14,205,900 − 14,205,090 checks this.

## Final check

**1 (2).** 6,409,008 ___ 6,490,008. **<**; ten-thousands decides.  
**2 (2).** Order 9,090,090; 9,009,900; 9,900,009 ascending. **9,009,900; 9,090,090; 9,900,009.**  
**3 (2).** Why is 10,000,001 > 9,999,999? **Eight digits versus seven; the first is over ten million and the second under it.**  
**4 (2).** Correct ascending order: 3,450,020; 3,405,200; 3,540,002. **3,405,200; 3,450,020; 3,540,002.**

**Teaching response:** For wrong comparisons, show the first different column. For wrong lists, show the first adjacent pair out of order. At 7–8 points, proceed; 5–6, retry a similar pair; below 5, revisit the worked comparisons.

**Illustration:** Two aligned place-value grids for 4,305,090 and 4,350,009 highlighting the ten-thousands difference. Alt text must describe the deciding digits. Cursor: keep narrative and answer feedback, preserve metadata, no reflections.

<a id="lesson-12"></a>

## Manuscript 12 — s3-mat-y5-w01-l4

**Existing title:** Number sense warm-ups and fluency  
**Source:** `docs/curriculum-authoring/lessons/s3-mat-y5-w01-l4.md`  
**Authoring status:** prose_draft_for_review, owner review pending.

# The number detective's training room

**Year 5 · Mathematics · Week 1 Lesson 4 · 55–60 minutes.** Keep existing attached outcomes.

## How to become fluent without rushing

A number detective needs to notice patterns, spot mistakes and decide whether an answer makes sense. Fluency is not simply answering as fast as possible. It means recognising the structure of a number so you can work accurately, then gradually becoming more efficient.

Suppose you see **5,090,004**. Instead of reading each digit separately, your eyes can recognise **5 million | 090 thousand | 004 ones**. That means five million, ninety thousand, four. You can also see that the 9 is worth 90,000. If someone says this number is smaller than 5,009,400, you can check the ten-thousands column and explain why that claim is false. These are different uses of the same understanding.

Today you'll move through four short challenges. Read the explanations carefully whenever a result surprises you.

## Challenge 1 — read and rebuild

Imagine an imaginary stadium reporting **7,204,016** visitors across many years. Say the number aloud before continuing. It is **seven million, two hundred and four thousand, sixteen**. The group 016 means sixteen, not one hundred and six.

Now write the expanded form: **7,000,000 + 200,000 + 4,000 + 10 + 6**. The zeros still matter in the numeral even though we omit zero terms from the addition.

A second report says **3,000,000 + 50,000 + 600 + 9**. Put the amounts in their columns. The result is **3,050,609**. If you wrote 3,50,609, remember that the thousands group must have three positions.

**Try independently:** Read 8,040,306. **Eight million, forty thousand, three hundred and six.** Expand 2,006,080. **2,000,000 + 6,000 + 80.** Rebuild 4,000,000 + 90,000 + 5,000 + 70 + 2. **4,095,072.**

## Challenge 2 — zeros are not decoration

A student writes **6,040,200 = 6,000,000 + 400,000 + 200**. What went wrong? The 4 is in the ten-thousands column, not the hundred-thousands column. The correct expansion is **6,000,000 + 40,000 + 200**.

This is a useful kind of mistake because it reveals *why* the zero before the 4 matters. Without it, the 4 could move into the wrong position and become ten times larger.

**Try:** Find the error in “9,003,050 is nine million, thirty thousand, fifty.” **Answer:** 003 is three thousand, not thirty thousand. The correct reading is **nine million, three thousand, fifty**.

**Try:** Find the error in “2,080,005 has eight hundred-thousands.” **Answer:** The 8 is in ten-thousands, so it is worth **80,000**.

## Challenge 3 — decide which is closer

Imagine a number line from 5,000,000 to 6,000,000. A marker is placed at **5,490,000**. Is it closer to five million or six million? It is 490,000 above five million and 510,000 below six million, so it is **closer to five million**. It is tempting to see the 49 and think it is nearly 50, but distances provide a reliable check.

Now compare **4,990,000** and **5,020,000** as candidates closest to 5,000,000. The first is 10,000 away; the second is 20,000 away. So **4,990,000** is closer, even though it is smaller.

**Try:** Which is closer to 8,500,000: 8,490,000 or 8,520,000? **8,490,000**; it is 10,000 away, compared with 20,000. Which is closer to 3,000,000: 2,980,000 or 3,050,000? **2,980,000**, 20,000 away versus 50,000.

## Challenge 4 — construct the number

Use the digits **0, 1, 3, 6, 8, 9** exactly once to make the greatest six-digit number. Put the greatest available digit in the largest place first. The answer is **986,310**.

For the smallest six-digit number, you cannot put 0 first: that would leave only five digits in the actual number. Start with the smallest nonzero digit, **1**, then use 0 and the remaining digits in ascending order. The answer is **103,689**.

**Try:** Make a number between 600,000 and 700,000 using each digit once. **Example:** 631,890. Any valid six-digit arrangement beginning with 6 is acceptable.

## Apply: the treasure ledger

Three imaginary stores report Hill Cave **8,406,250** tokens, River Vault **8,460,205**, and Forest Cache **8,406,520**.

Arrange them from smallest to largest. **Hill Cave, Forest Cache, River Vault.** Notice how the first two tie through thousands and differ at hundreds.

How many more tokens are in Forest Cache than Hill Cave? Subtract 8,406,520 − 8,406,250 to get **270**.

Is River Vault closer to 8,400,000 or 8,500,000? Its distances are **60,205** and **39,795**, so it is closer to **8,500,000**.

A sign claims that all three contain more than 8.45 million tokens. Convert 8.45 million to **8,450,000**. Only River Vault exceeds that amount. The sign is **false**.

## Final check

**1 (2).** Expand 9,008,070. **9,000,000 + 8,000 + 70.**  
**2 (2).** Rebuild 4,000,000 + 300,000 + 90 + 2. **4,300,092.**  
**3 (2).** Order 5,050,500; 5,500,050; 5,005,500 ascending. **5,005,500; 5,050,500; 5,500,050.**  
**4 (2).** Correct “6,070,000 has seven hundred-thousands.” **It has seven ten-thousands, worth 70,000.**

A correct explanation matters as much as a correct symbol. At 7–8 marks, proceed; 5–6, practise the missed skill; below 5, revisit the grouped-number chart and work through examples with support.

**Visual:** A labelled number line from 8,400,000 to 8,500,000 with River Vault at 8,460,205, proportionally placed. Alt text states both distances. Cursor: preserve explanatory prose and feedback, save responses, no reflections.

<a id="lesson-13"></a>

## Manuscript 13 — s3-mat-y5-w02-l1

**Existing title:** Rounding to the nearest ten, hundred and thousand  
**Source:** `docs/curriculum-authoring/lessons/s3-mat-y5-w02-l1.md`  
**Authoring status:** prose_draft_for_review, owner review pending.

# When an exact number is more than you need

**Year 5 · Mathematics · Week 2 Lesson 1 · Concept · Approximately 60 minutes.** Preserve existing learning outcomes and lesson identity. No reflections.

## An enormous crowd

Imagine a wildlife park counted **38,746 visitors** last month. A manager preparing an accurate financial report must use 38,746. But a newspaper headline might reasonably say **about 39,000 visitors**. Neither statement is necessarily wrong. They serve different purposes: one is exact; the other gives a useful approximation.

**Rounding** means replacing a number with a nearby number at a chosen place value. The rounded number is not secretly equal to the original. It is an estimate that is easier to understand or calculate with. Always notice the place you are rounding to: 38,746 rounds differently to the nearest ten, hundred and thousand.

## Why the halfway point matters

Picture a number line from 40 to 50. The number 43 is three steps from 40 and seven from 50, so its nearest multiple of ten is **40**. The number 48 is eight steps from 40 but only two from 50, so its nearest multiple of ten is **50**. What about 45? It is exactly halfway. The usual school convention for rounding whole numbers is that halfway values round **up**, so 45 rounds to 50.

This is why we inspect the digit **immediately to the right** of the place being rounded. When it is 0, 1, 2, 3 or 4, the chosen digit stays the same. When it is 5, 6, 7, 8 or 9, the chosen digit increases by one. Every digit to the right becomes zero. This rule is a shortcut for comparing distances to the two neighbouring multiples.

## Nearest ten

Round **6,483** to the nearest ten. The neighbouring multiples of ten are **6,480** and **6,490**. The number is three away from 6,480 and seven away from 6,490, so **6,480** is nearer. Look at the ones digit, 3, to make the decision.

Now round **6,487**. It is seven above 6,480 and three below 6,490. The ones digit 7 tells us to round up to **6,490**.

A common mistake is changing the tens digit but leaving the ones digit untouched: writing 6,493 for the second problem. That is not rounding to the nearest ten, because a rounded multiple of ten must end in **0**.

## Nearest hundred

Round **27,649** to the nearest hundred. The neighbouring hundreds are **27,600** and **27,700**. The halfway point is 27,650. Because 27,649 is just below halfway, the answer is **27,600**. We look at the tens digit, 4, because it is immediately to the right of the hundreds column.

For **27,650**, the school rounding convention gives **27,700**. A difference of just one between 27,649 and 27,650 changes the rounded result. This is not a flaw in rounding: the numbers lie on opposite sides of the boundary.

## Nearest thousand

Consider **38,746** again. The neighbouring thousands are 38,000 and 39,000. The halfway point is 38,500. Because 38,746 is above that point, the number rounds to **39,000**. The hundreds digit, 7, signals that we round up.

Try **64,499**. It is 499 above 64,000 and 501 below 65,000, so it rounds to **64,000**. Even though the last three digits look large, they are still below the halfway mark of 500.

Now round **99,750** to the nearest thousand. The answer is **100,000**. Rounding can create a new digit in a larger place. If you wrote 99,000, you overlooked that 750 is past halfway.

## Guided practice: explain the boundary

**1.** Round 5,284 to the nearest ten. The ones digit is 4; the answer is **5,280**. The neighbouring tens are 5,280 and 5,290.

**2.** Round 5,284 to the nearest hundred. The tens digit is 8; the answer is **5,300**. Notice that the same original number has a different approximation when the requested place changes.

**3.** Round 5,284 to the nearest thousand. The hundreds digit is 2; the answer is **5,000**.

**4.** Round 14,950 to the nearest hundred. It lies halfway between 14,900 and 15,000, so the answer is **15,000**.

**5.** Explain why 4,450 rounds to 4,500 to the nearest hundred but to 4,000 to the nearest thousand. **The halfway boundary for hundreds is 4,450; for thousands it is 4,500. The number is at the first boundary but below the second.**

## Independent practice

Give the rounded value and, for questions A–D, the two neighbouring multiples.

**A.** 7,236 to nearest ten: **7,240**, between 7,230 and 7,240.  
**B.** 7,236 to nearest hundred: **7,200**, between 7,200 and 7,300.  
**C.** 7,236 to nearest thousand: **7,000**, between 7,000 and 8,000.  
**D.** 48,550 to nearest hundred: **48,600**, between 48,500 and 48,600.  
**E.** 12,499 to nearest thousand: **12,000**.  
**F.** 12,500 to nearest thousand: **13,000**.  
**G.** 999,501 to nearest thousand: **1,000,000**.  
**H.** 340,049 to nearest hundred: **340,000**.  
**I.** A student rounds 6,876 to nearest ten and writes 6,880, then claims the result is exact. What is wrong? **The rounded value is correct, but it is an approximation; the exact count remains 6,876.**

## Application: a museum report

An imaginary museum recorded **124,587 visitors**. Its ticketing system needs the exact number. A poster aimed at the public could say **about 125,000 visitors** to the nearest thousand. A planning discussion might use **about 124,600** to the nearest hundred. Both approximations are reasonable if clearly labelled, but the museum must not use rounded values to reconcile exact ticket sales.

**Task:** Explain which figure belongs in a financial ledger and which could appear in a headline. **Model:** Use 124,587 in the ledger because every visitor matters to the exact record; use approximately 125,000 in the headline because a rounded total communicates scale.

## Final assessment — 10 points

**1 (2).** Round 83,746 to the nearest ten and hundred. **83,750 and 83,700.**  
**2 (2).** Round 83,746 to the nearest thousand. **84,000.**  
**3 (2).** Explain why 65,499 rounds down to 65,000 to the nearest thousand. **It is below the halfway point of 65,500.**  
**4 (2).** Round 999,500 to the nearest thousand. **1,000,000.**  
**5 (2).** Explain the difference between an exact count and a rounded estimate. **An exact count states the recorded amount; a rounded estimate replaces it with a nearby, simpler number.**

**Targeted feedback:** If the student rounds using the wrong digit, identify the requested place and circle the digit immediately right. If trailing digits are not zero, show the two neighbouring multiples. If 99,750 becomes 99,000, draw the number line to 100,000.

**Visual specification:** Three aligned number lines for 5,284 rounded to ten, hundred and thousand; halfway points labelled. Cursor: retain all teaching prose, answer explanations and assessment; no reflections.

<a id="lesson-14"></a>

## Manuscript 14 — s3-mat-y5-w02-l2

**Existing title:** Estimating with rounded numbers  
**Source:** `docs/curriculum-authoring/lessons/s3-mat-y5-w02-l2.md`  
**Authoring status:** prose_draft_for_review, owner review pending.

# Is the answer sensible before you calculate?

**Year 5 · Mathematics · Week 2 Lesson 2 · Strategies · Approximately 60 minutes.** Keep existing outcomes and identity. No reflections.

## The shopping-list problem

A school is buying equipment. Three items cost **$298**, **$403** and **$197**. Before adding carefully, you might notice that these are close to **$300**, **$400** and **$200**. That makes an estimated total of **$900**. The exact sum is **$898**, so the estimate is extremely useful.

Estimating does not mean guessing without thinking. It means choosing simpler nearby numbers and calculating with them to judge the likely size of a result. An estimate can help plan a budget, detect a calculator mistake or decide whether there is enough time or space for something.

## Why different estimates can both be useful

Round each price to the nearest hundred and you get $300 + $400 + $200 = **$900**. Round to the nearest ten and you get $300 + $400 + $200 again in this case. But with other values, the choice matters.

Consider **347 + 268**. Rounding to the nearest hundred gives 300 + 300 = **600**. Rounding to the nearest ten gives 350 + 270 = **620**. The exact answer is **615**. Both estimates communicate the approximate size, but 620 is closer. Greater precision can be useful, although it may require more mental effort.

An estimate is not automatically better just because it uses more digits. Its usefulness depends on the decision you are making. If you only need to know whether a total is around six hundred or six thousand, rounding to hundreds is enough.

## Rounding can push an estimate above or below

A common misconception is that rounding always makes numbers larger. It does not. We round some values up and others down. For example, 248 rounds to 200 to the nearest hundred, while 276 rounds to 300. Their estimated sum is 500; the exact sum is 524. The estimate is below the exact value because the combined effects of rounding happen to reduce it.

Look at **449 + 449**. Rounding both to the nearest hundred gives 400 + 400 = **800**, whereas the exact sum is **898**. That is still a rough estimate, but it is not especially close. Rounding to the nearest ten gives 450 + 450 = **900**, much nearer. When the answer needs to be more precise, choose an appropriate place value.

## Estimate before using written methods

Imagine calculating **6,842 − 2,915**. Round to the nearest thousand: 7,000 − 3,000 = **4,000**. The exact difference is **3,927**. If a calculator displays 39,270, the estimate immediately reveals that something has gone wrong. The result should be near four thousand, not forty thousand.

Estimation is especially useful for checking multiplication. **49 × 21** is close to **50 × 20 = 1,000**. The exact product is **1,029**. If someone reports 10,290, their answer is about ten times too large.

For division, **1,198 ÷ 6** is close to **1,200 ÷ 6 = 200**. The exact answer is approximately 199.7. This gives a reasonable expectation before carrying out division.

## Guided estimation

**1.** Estimate 397 + 602 by rounding to hundreds. **400 + 600 = 1,000**. Exact total: **999**. The estimate is one higher.

**2.** Estimate 5,920 − 2,140 to nearest thousands. **6,000 − 2,000 = 4,000**. Exact difference: **3,780**.

**3.** Estimate 198 × 4 by rounding 198 to 200. **200 × 4 = 800**. Exact answer: **792**.

**4.** A classmate says 4,780 + 3,190 = 79,700. Is that sensible? **No.** Rounding to thousands gives 5,000 + 3,000 = about **8,000**; the proposed answer is far too large.

**5.** Why might nearest tens be better than nearest hundreds for 449 + 449? **It produces 900, which is much closer to the exact 898 than 800.**

## Independent practice

For each calculation, first write an estimate, then compare with the exact answer provided. Your estimate may vary if you choose and explain a sensible rounding place.

**A.** 503 + 796. **Estimate:** 500 + 800 = **1,300**. **Exact:** 1,299.  
**B.** 8,104 − 3,898. **Estimate:** 8,100 − 3,900 = **4,200**. **Exact:** 4,206.  
**C.** 62 × 19. **Estimate:** 60 × 20 = **1,200**. **Exact:** 1,178.  
**D.** 3,015 ÷ 5. **Estimate:** 3,000 ÷ 5 = **600**. **Exact:** 603.  
**E.** 2,487 + 4,512. **Estimate:** 2,500 + 4,500 = **7,000**. **Exact:** 6,999.  
**F.** 9,999 − 4,996. **Estimate:** 10,000 − 5,000 = **5,000**. **Exact:** 5,003.

**G.** A delivery company expects 203 parcels per day for five days. Estimate the weekly total. **About 200 × 5 = 1,000 parcels.** Exact total if each day has 203: **1,015**.

**H.** Which is the better estimate for 449 + 449: 800 or 900? **900**, because the exact result is 898. Explain that the more suitable rounding place produces the closer estimate.

## Application: a community garden

A fictional community garden needs **387 litres** of water for one bed, **412 litres** for another and **194 litres** for a third. Its tank holds **1,000 litres**. Estimate the total first: 400 + 400 + 200 = **1,000 litres**. That estimate suggests the tank might be just enough, but it cannot prove it.

Now calculate exactly: 387 + 412 = 799; 799 + 194 = **993 litres**. The tank is enough, with **7 litres** remaining. This example shows why estimating is useful for planning but exact calculation is necessary when a decision depends on a small margin.

**Question:** Would it be safe to claim the tank has 100 litres left based on the estimate? **No.** The estimate only shows the total is close to 1,000, and the exact remaining amount is 7 litres.

## Final assessment

**1 (2).** Estimate 697 + 304 by rounding to hundreds. **700 + 300 = 1,000.**  
**2 (2).** Estimate 81 × 12 using friendly numbers. **80 × 10 = 800** is a rough estimate; **80 × 12 = 960** is closer to the exact **972**. Accept sensible explained choices.  
**3 (2).** Explain how estimating detects an error in 4,900 − 2,100 = 28,000. **The difference should be about 3,000, not 28,000.**  
**4 (2).** Estimate 2,999 ÷ 3. **3,000 ÷ 3 = 1,000.**  
**5 (2).** Explain why the garden needed an exact calculation. **The estimate was near tank capacity, so a small difference determined whether enough water remained.**

**Feedback:** If an estimate is implausibly far away, check whether the learner rounded to a useful place. If the estimate is presented as exact, distinguish the approximation from the actual computation. **Visual:** Side-by-side estimation and exact calculation for garden water, with a 1,000-litre capacity bar. Cursor: implement both estimate and exact answer fields, preserve teaching, no reflections.

<a id="lesson-15"></a>

## Manuscript 15 — s3-mat-y5-w02-l3

**Existing title:** Number lines and large number patterns  
**Source:** `docs/curriculum-authoring/lessons/s3-mat-y5-w02-l3.md`  
**Authoring status:** prose_draft_for_review, owner review pending.

# The invisible distances between enormous numbers

**Year 5 · Mathematics · Week 2 Lesson 3 · Problem solving · 60 minutes.** Preserve attached outcomes. No reflections.

## A number line is a map of distance

A number line is not simply a row of numbers. Its positions show **how far apart** values are. The space between 10 and 20 must be the same as the space between 20 and 30 if the scale is uniform. The same rule applies to large numbers: a line from 2,000,000 to 3,000,000 can represent a million-unit journey.

Imagine a number line with 2,000,000 at the left end and 3,000,000 at the right end. The midpoint is **2,500,000** because it is 500,000 away from either end. A point at 2,750,000 lies three-quarters of the way across: it is 750,000 above the left end and 250,000 below the right.

If you place 2,750,000 exactly in the middle, the drawing would be misleading even if the number label were correct. A good number line respects the scale.

## Find the interval before the missing number

Suppose five equally spaced marks read:

**4,000,000 — ? — ? — ? — 5,000,000**

There are **four equal intervals**, not five. The total difference is 1,000,000. Divide by four to get **250,000 per interval**. The missing labels are **4,250,000; 4,500,000; 4,750,000**.

A common error is dividing by the number of marks rather than the number of gaps. Count the spaces between the ticks. Five marks create four gaps.

## Position a number between labelled ticks

A number line has 6,000,000 and 6,500,000 at its ends. Where does **6,125,000** belong? The full distance is 500,000. The target is 125,000 above the left end. Because 125,000 is one quarter of 500,000, place the point **one quarter of the way across**.

Now locate **6,375,000**. It is 375,000 above the left end, or three quarters of the distance. This approach is more reliable than placing numbers by eye alone.

## Patterns can grow or shrink

Look at **120,000; 145,000; 170,000; 195,000**. Each term increases by **25,000**. To find the next term, add the same amount: **220,000**. The rule is not “add 25” because the place value matters.

Now examine **9,000,000; 8,750,000; 8,500,000; 8,250,000**. Each step subtracts **250,000**, so the next term is **8,000,000**. A pattern can decrease as well as increase.

Not every pattern uses addition. Consider **125,000; 250,000; 500,000; 1,000,000**. Each term is **double** the previous term. The next term is **2,000,000**. Always compare consecutive terms to determine whether the relationship is additive or multiplicative.

## Guided examples

**1.** A line from 1,000,000 to 1,800,000 has four equal intervals. How large is each? Difference 800,000 ÷ 4 = **200,000**. Labels: 1,000,000; 1,200,000; 1,400,000; 1,600,000; 1,800,000.

**2.** Which is nearer 5,000,000: 4,850,000 or 5,300,000? Distances are 150,000 and 300,000, so **4,850,000** is nearer.

**3.** Continue 3,050,000; 3,150,000; 3,250,000. **3,350,000; 3,450,000**, adding 100,000.

**4.** Continue 6,400,000; 6,200,000; 6,000,000. **5,800,000; 5,600,000**, subtracting 200,000.

**5.** Explain why 2,400,000 should not be placed halfway between 2,000,000 and 3,000,000. **The midpoint is 2,500,000; 2,400,000 is only 40% of the way across.**

## Independent problems

**A.** Six equally spaced marks run from 3,000,000 to 4,000,000. Find the step. **200,000**, because five intervals share 1,000,000.

**B.** Fill the marks: 7,000,000; ___; ___; 7,750,000, equally spaced. **7,250,000; 7,500,000.**

**C.** On a line from 4,000,000 to 4,800,000, where is 4,600,000? **Three quarters of the way from the left.**

**D.** Continue 980,000; 1,030,000; 1,080,000. **1,130,000; 1,180,000.**

**E.** Continue 4,000,000; 2,000,000; 1,000,000. **500,000; 250,000**, halving each time.

**F.** A student labels equally spaced ticks 2,000,000; 2,100,000; 2,300,000; 2,400,000. Identify the error. **The differences are 100,000, 200,000, 100,000, so the labels cannot belong to equally spaced ticks.**

**G.** Write one number between 6,750,000 and 6,800,000 that is a multiple of 10,000. **Examples:** 6,760,000; 6,770,000; 6,780,000; 6,790,000.

## Application: a fictional train route

An imaginary train route is **800 kilometres** long. The planning map uses a number line marked at 0, 200, 400, 600 and 800 kilometres. A station is at kilometre **350**. It belongs between 200 and 400, **three quarters of the way from 200 toward 400**, because 350 is 150 of the 200 kilometres across.

Now imagine a budget number line from **$2,000,000 to $2,800,000**, with the same five-tick layout. Each interval represents **$200,000**, not 200 kilometres. The mathematical structure is identical even though the units change.

**Question:** Where would a budget of $2,350,000 sit? **Between $2,200,000 and $2,400,000, three quarters of the way across that interval.** Explain by calculating 150,000 ÷ 200,000 = 3/4.

## Final assessment — 10 points

**1 (2).** Four equal intervals between 5,000,000 and 6,000,000: what is the step? **250,000.**  
**2 (2).** Give the midpoint of 7,200,000 and 7,800,000. **7,500,000.**  
**3 (2).** Continue 2,150,000; 2,300,000; 2,450,000. **2,600,000; 2,750,000.**  
**4 (2).** Continue 800,000; 1,600,000; 3,200,000. **6,400,000; 12,800,000.**  
**5 (2).** Explain why five tick marks create four intervals. **Intervals are the spaces between adjacent marks; there is one fewer gap than marks.**

**Feedback:** If a step is wrong, count gaps before dividing. If a plotted point is misplaced, compare its distance from each endpoint. If a pattern rule fails, test it against every adjacent pair.

**Visual:** Accurate evenly scaled number lines showing 4,000,000–5,000,000 in four intervals and 6,000,000–6,500,000 with quarter points. Cursor: implement accessible number-line input or labelled selection, not arbitrary pixel-only dragging. No reflections.

<a id="lesson-16"></a>

## Manuscript 16 — s3-mat-y5-w02-l4

**Existing title:** Quick check: place value and rounding  
**Source:** `docs/curriculum-authoring/lessons/s3-mat-y5-w02-l4.md`  
**Authoring status:** prose_draft_for_review, owner review pending.

# The number detective's second case

**Year 5 · Mathematics · Week 2 Lesson 4 · Fluency and review · 60 minutes.** Keep existing outcome metadata. No reflections.

## One number, many ways to understand it

The imaginary National Discovery Archive records **6,485,270** specimens. That numeral is not merely a string of digits. It tells us how many millions, hundred-thousands, ten-thousands, thousands, hundreds, tens and ones there are. We can read it, expand it, compare it with another count and round it to communicate its approximate size.

A fluent mathematician moves between these forms without losing the meaning of the number. Today you will solve a connected set of problems, using the methods taught earlier in the unit. When you make a mistake, use the explanation to find the precise step that went wrong.

## Case 1: decode the archive number

Read **6,485,270** aloud: **six million, four hundred and eighty-five thousand, two hundred and seventy**. The groups are 6 | 485 | 270. The middle group is not forty-eight thousand; it is four hundred and eighty-five thousand.

Expand the number: **6,000,000 + 400,000 + 80,000 + 5,000 + 200 + 70**. The 8 is worth **80,000** because it is in the ten-thousands column. The 7 is worth **70**, not seven hundred.

**Try:** Expand 4,070,609. **Answer:** 4,000,000 + 70,000 + 600 + 9. Rebuild 8,000,000 + 200,000 + 3,000 + 40 + 5. **Answer:** 8,203,045.

## Case 2: which record is larger?

A second archive records **6,458,720** specimens. Compare it with 6,485,270. Both have six million and four hundred thousand. At the ten-thousands place, the first has **8** and the second has **5**, so **6,485,270 > 6,458,720**. You do not need to compare the hundreds or tens once you find the first difference from the left.

**Try:** Place these in ascending order: 3,405,080; 3,450,008; 3,405,800. **Answer:** 3,405,080; 3,405,800; 3,450,008.

**Error detective:** A student says 7,090,010 is smaller than 7,009,100 because 100 is greater than 010. The comparison is wrong because it begins at the right-hand groups. The first differing place from the left is ten-thousands: 9 > 0, so **7,090,010 is larger**.

## Case 3: communicate a useful estimate

Round 6,485,270 to the nearest thousand. The hundreds digit is 2, so the answer is **6,485,000**. To the nearest hundred thousand, inspect the ten-thousands digit 8 and round up: **6,500,000**. To the nearest million, inspect the hundred-thousands digit 4 and round down: **6,000,000**.

These three estimates serve different purposes. A scientific inventory might use the exact total. A short public summary might use approximately 6.5 million, but that is a decimal representation of the rounded hundred-thousand value, not a claim that the original count was exactly 6,500,000.

**Try:** Round 8,749,500 to the nearest thousand: **8,750,000**. To the nearest hundred thousand: **8,700,000**, because the ten-thousands digit is 4. To the nearest million: **9,000,000**, because the hundred-thousands digit is 7.

## Case 4: estimate and check

A museum expects **4,902** visitors on Saturday and **5,087** on Sunday. Rounding to thousands gives 5,000 + 5,000 = **10,000**. The exact total is **9,989**, so the estimate is very close.

If a student calculates 99,890, the estimate shows a place-value error. A result around one hundred thousand cannot be sensible when each daily count is only around five thousand.

**Try:** Estimate 7,890 − 2,110 to the nearest thousand. **8,000 − 2,000 = 6,000**. The exact difference is **5,780**. The estimate is useful for checking scale.

## Case 5: patterns and number lines

An archive reports these yearly totals: **2,250,000; 2,500,000; 2,750,000; 3,000,000**. Each year increases by **250,000**. The next value would be **3,250,000** if the pattern continued.

Now imagine a number line from 2,000,000 to 3,000,000 with four equal intervals. Each interval is **250,000**. The same numbers can label successive marks on that line, except the line begins at 2,000,000. Recognising the connection between sequences and evenly spaced number lines makes patterns easier to verify.

**Try:** A line from 5,000,000 to 6,200,000 has three equal intervals. What is the step? **400,000**, because 1,200,000 ÷ 3 = 400,000.

## Independent archive challenge

Write answers before checking the key.

**A.** What is the value of 9 in 3,092,040? **90,000.**  
**B.** Expand 5,006,700. **5,000,000 + 6,000 + 700.**  
**C.** Rebuild 7,000,000 + 400,000 + 20,000 + 8. **7,420,008.**  
**D.** Compare 4,900,005 and 4,090,500. **4,900,005 > 4,090,500.**  
**E.** Round 27,649 to the nearest hundred. **27,600.**  
**F.** Round 27,650 to the nearest hundred. **27,700.**  
**G.** Estimate 398 + 601. **400 + 600 = 1,000**; exact 999.  
**H.** Continue 4,400,000; 4,650,000; 4,900,000. **5,150,000; 5,400,000.**  
**I.** Find the midpoint of 8,000,000 and 9,000,000. **8,500,000.**  
**J.** Explain why 99,600 rounds to 100,000 to the nearest thousand. **It is beyond the halfway point 99,500 between 99,000 and 100,000.**

## A connected real-world decision

A fictional conservation program counted **1,249,650** seedlings planted in Region A and **1,251,080** in Region B. A headline says “Both regions planted about 1.25 million seedlings.” That is reasonable as a broad description, but the two exact totals are different.

Which region planted more? **Region B**, because both have one million and two hundred thousand, but Region B has five ten-thousands while Region A has four. What is the difference? Subtract 1,251,080 − 1,249,650 = **1,430 seedlings**.

Round each count to the nearest thousand: Region A becomes **1,250,000** and Region B becomes **1,251,000**. These rounded values suggest a difference of 1,000, not the exact 1,430. This illustrates why rounded numbers are useful for communication but should not replace exact values when the difference itself matters.

## Final assessment — 12 points

**1 (2).** Expand 9,080,406. **9,000,000 + 80,000 + 400 + 6.**  
**2 (2).** Rebuild 6,000,000 + 300,000 + 7,000 + 20. **6,307,020.**  
**3 (2).** Order 5,050,500; 5,500,050; 5,005,500 ascending. **5,005,500; 5,050,500; 5,500,050.**  
**4 (2).** Round 48,550 to the nearest hundred and thousand. **48,600; 49,000.**  
**5 (2).** Estimate 3,980 + 6,110 to the nearest hundred. **4,000 + 6,100 = 10,100.**  
**6 (2).** Continue 2,100,000; 2,350,000; 2,600,000. **2,850,000; 3,100,000.**

**Diagnostic feedback:** Errors in 1–2 suggest reviewing place-value columns. Errors in 3 suggest comparing from the left. Errors in 4 suggest checking halfway boundaries. Errors in 5 suggest selecting a consistent rounding place. Errors in 6 suggest calculating the difference between neighbouring terms. A score of 10–12 indicates readiness; 7–9 calls for targeted retry; 0–6 calls for supported re-teaching of the relevant cases.

**Visual:** Archive number 6,485,270 in a labelled place-value grid; a number line with 250,000 intervals. Cursor: keep all explanations, interactive tasks and diagnostic feedback; no reflections.
